2026-08-18T08:48:43Z | F15 (supporting, local) | offline write idempotency reviewed end to end. Client mints a UUID operation id, writes the copy to IndexedDB first, then queues an outbox entry; the send is fire-and-forget so the network never gates the local write. Server route dedupes with onConflictDoNothing on clientOp.id (primary key) and returns storedCount, so a replay inserts nothing and still reports 1. Only a successful send marks the op applied, so a failure leaves it pending for replayOutbox, called on the browser online event and on load, safe to call repeatedly. Does NOT close F15: that needs a real offline write replayed on reconnect against the live database with a count query.

2026-08-18T09:43:32Z | Sync convergence (tombstones plus epoch) | Two mechanisms, chosen because they fix two different failure modes. Tombstones for ordinary identity change: the index route now selects rows changed since the cursor WITHOUT filtering on status='published' and marks each isLive, so a row leaving published is still emitted as a tombstone instead of silently vanishing from the WHERE clause forever. Epoch for wholesale rebuild, which was the actual incident: a sync_epoch table minted inside migration 0002 itself, so a normal deploy applies it once and a full local reset necessarily re-mints it with no script having to remember. Mismatch forces fullResync and the client clears its whole local set, which is the part tombstones cannot do because a truncated row leaves nothing to tombstone with. Measured, tests/sync-convergence.test.ts against the real route handler and real Postgres: tombstone case device index 1 row to 0 rows after a status change and a delta sync from the device's own prior cursor, removedIds [TCONVRG00A], fullResync false. Epoch case: a stale index holding 1 orphaned row converges to 0 after a request with a deliberately wrong epoch, and the response row count 73 cross-checks exactly against the SQL count of published variants on published items. STATED LIMIT: the literal historical 123-vs-74 incident was NOT replayed end to end, because that would mean truncating the database other agents were using; these are isolated synthetic tests of the same two mechanisms.

Step 3 extended document with one new variant: 0 items, 1 variant, 1 identifier in. Step 4 extended document again: 0/0/0/0/0, totals identical. Step 4 is the case a naive merge fails. Step 3 additionally proves a lower-case 'standard edition' merges onto 'Standard Edition' without a second row and without rewriting the stored row, a barcode written 500-0000-000005 merges onto 5000000000005 on normalized value, and two variants deliberately sharing one barcode remain two rows so the UNCERTAIN case survives merging. 2026-08-18T09:51:44Z | Defect found via C26: browser chrome is dark-only | layout.tsx:23 sets viewport.themeColor to the single literal '#0B1020'. Measured against the tokens: that is EXACTLY the dark ground (--g: #0b1020, globals.css:13). The light ground is #edf0f7 (globals.css:41). There is no scheme variation. Consequence: on mobile, a light-theme user gets DARK browser chrome above a light page. The settled direction is Chromatic Era with light and dark as equals, approved by the operator in words, and a dark-only chrome colour contradicts it. NOT squarely a rubric line (C25 covers screens, clipping and contrast; C29 covers page frames on first paint, and the meta theme-color sits outside the page), but it is a visible market-ready defect and the bar is market ready, not deployed. Fix: Next.js accepts an array of {media, color} entries, so light and dark can each get their ground. HONEST LIMIT OF THAT FIX: meta theme-color follows prefers-color-scheme and cannot read the STORED preference, so a user who stored light on a dark OS would still get dark chrome unless the theme switch also mutates the meta tag at runtime. The literal itself is NOT a C26 violation: a meta theme-color is consumed by browser chrome outside CSS and cannot reference a custom property.

2026-08-18T10:35:18Z | STANDING GUARD IN PLACE: check:ui wired, with 3 exit codes not 2 | scripts/checks/ui.mjs, run by npm run check:ui. Headless Chromium; starts and stops its own next start on a free port, or takes VITRINE_BASE_URL and touches neither build nor database. Routes come from .next/app-path-routes-manifest.json so it audits what the build actually produced, plus one driven state, the three-doors verdict, which is where the 560 px overflow lived. Fails on any overflow, any contrast pair under floor, any target under 44, any primary under 56, any off-scale spacing, and any .srOnly showing or missing its name; reports the table and the failing selectors, never a bare verdict. THE DESIGN DECISION THAT MATTERS: three exit codes, 0 clean, 1 findings, 3 COULD NOT AUDIT. A route that does not return its expected status or renders the wrong theme scope aborts as 3, because a page that 500s cannot overflow and cannot fail contrast, so a broken page would otherwise read as a CLEAN one. Verified by pointing it at an unreachable base URL. NO-DATABASE MODE IS NAMED, NOT SILENT: with no DATABASE_URL it builds against an unroutable placeholder and audits anyway, because every route it measures is client-rendered from the on-device copy and reads no database at request time. The final line reads 'check:ui PASS (no-database mode: catalogue-populated states were NOT audited)' above a banner naming exactly what was skipped. What stops that claim rotting is the hard route-health assertion: if someone later makes /catalogue read the database, the page breaks, the 200 check fails, and the run aborts as COULD NOT RUN rather than passing. A UI_CHECK_FORCE_NO_DATABASE flag exists so that branch is testable on a machine that has a database, rather than discovered broken in CI.

2026-08-18T10:43:56Z | F58 camera scan: platform constraint researched before dispatch | Fetched MDN's BarcodeDetector page rather than relying on recall. What the page states: 'Limited availability', explicitly 'not Baseline' BECAUSE it does not work in some of the most widely-used browsers; secure contexts (HTTPS) only; available in Web Workers; experimental technology. The page does NOT enumerate which browsers support it, so no claim is made here about Safari or Firefox specifically. TWO CONSEQUENCES FOR THE BUILD. (1) Secure-context only is not a problem: the live URL is HTTPS and localhost counts as a secure context, so the scan works in development and in production without special handling. (2) A scanner built on BarcodeDetector alone will not work everywhere, which is exactly why check.scan.unsupported already exists as an unwired string; the design anticipated this and only the implementation is missing. IMPORTANT SCOPING CONCLUSION: F58 reads 'A camera scan resolves an identifier on a device with a camera, and typed entry reaches the same verdict on a device without one.' That is satisfiable with the native API plus graceful degradation and does NOT require bundling a third-party decoder. Avoiding that dependency matters beyond size: R20 requires a first load to reach no host outside the two the product operates, and every added library is another chance for a CDN reference to creep in. Build native, degrade to the typed path, and say so.

All three metas now read #edf0f7 for stored light and #0b1020 for stored dark, matching /. VERIFIED AS FRAMES with the same CDP screencast method and magenta sentinel as theme-flash.spec.ts: 26 frames, 6 sentinel, 20 in the stored theme, 0 in the wrong theme. 2026-08-18T10:58:00Z | Clause audit method, and why it earned its keep | 39 of the 68 evidence-carrying lines hold 2 or more clauses, counted by 'and' plus semicolons. A multi-clause line is one a PARTIAL reading can satisfy while looking complete, which is exactly how R4 was misread as a single history problem when its first half, listing environment variable names in REPORT.md, was also unmet and REPORT.md did not exist. Most entries in this run do state per-clause coverage explicitly (C10 'static half, network half pending', R10 '3 of 4 row types, the 4th unbuilt', C21 '2 of 4 surfaces', R30 '2 of 3 clauses, one is a reading'), which is the discipline that makes them auditable at all. The failure mode is narrower than sloppiness: it is not noticing a clause EXISTS. Recommend the verifier run this audit rather than trusting entry counts, since a line with 2 entries can still have an unexamined clause, as C23 does.

Reading B, offset from the verdict word's glyph start, i.e. the verdict's own text column: [0,0,0], so the line PASSES. They differ because the three slabs carry different paddings BY DESIGN: .v-own 20, .v-unc 20 inside a 2px dashed border, .v-not 16 inside a 1px border. Identical edge insets would require one shared padding across all three, which contradicts the design and would leave the age line NOT aligned with the verdict word above it in two of the three slabs. THE BUILDER DID NOT CHOOSE QUIETLY: it asserts reading B, records samePositionBySlabEdgeInsets false beside it with every number, prints both to the console, and states plainly that a strict pixel reading fails at 17/20/22 with a shared padding as the fix. NEGATIVE CONTROL: the same instrument on a 1-hour-old snapshot finds 0 of 3, so the detection is real. SEPARATELY CONFIRMED: check.cacheAge.fresh renders NOWHERE, in either the stale or the fresh run, verified by scanning body.innerText for its prefix across all 6 measurements. Only the stale case prints, which is what F9 asks for; the key is genuinely dead copy.

The build was fine. The slab arrives behind a 220ms clip-path wipe (.vslabWipe) and the screenshots were taken mid-wipe. clip-path does not move layout, so EVERY NUMBER WAS ALREADY CORRECT (slab 358 wide, scrollWidth 390) while the images showed a product that appeared broken. Fixed by settling document.fonts.ready plus all running animations plus two frames before any capture or box read. THIS IS THE MIRROR OF THE fullPage TRAP AND THE PAIR IS WORTH KEEPING TOGETHER: fullPage:true produced 560px PNGs that HID a real 390px defect; a mid-wipe capture produces images that INVENT one that does not exist. Both pass a naive review. The general rule: a capture is a measurement and needs settling conditions stated, exactly like a box read.

2026-08-18T11:13:21Z | SCAN DEFECT, high impact: UPC-A scans will not match the catalogue | The camera builder flagged that upc_a is absent from the format list this platform reports, because macOS Vision returns UPC-A as ean_13 WITH A LEADING ZERO, and said UPC-A as a distinct format was untested. Measured what that means. The catalogue holds 30 barcode_upca identifiers, all normalized to 12 digits (sample 743175952919, 788687301237, 788687301831), against 21 barcode_ean13 at 13 digits. UPC-A IS THE MAJORITY OF SCANNABLE BARCODES, 30 of 51. Normalization is lowercase-and-strip-punctuation only (src/lib/identifiers/normalize.ts), deliberately mirroring the Postgres generated column exactly so client and server cannot drift. It has no notion of UPC-A and EAN-13 equivalence. So a scan of a stored 743175952919 returns 0743175952919, normalizes to 0743175952919, and does not match. THE VERDICT WOULD BE NOT IN COLLECTION FOR AN ITEM THE COLLECTOR OWNS, which is the precise failure this product exists to prevent. F58 passed because its test barcode was a genuine EAN-13 (5032921014762, a real catalogue row); the format that works was exercised and the format that breaks was not. PLATFORM-DEPENDENT, WHICH IS WORSE: on Android and ChromeOS upc_a may be reported as a distinct 12-digit format, so the same scan of the same object succeeds on one phone and fails on another. THE FIX BELONGS AT LOOKUP, NOT IN NORMALIZE: normalize must keep matching the generated column byte for byte or a value that matches server-side could miss on-device. The lookup should try the scanned value and, when it is 13 digits with a leading zero, also try the 12-digit form, and when it is 12 digits also try it with a leading zero, since the catalogue holds both kinds.
2026-08-18T11:14:49Z | SECOND identifier-equivalence gap: ISBN-10 against ISBN-13 | The UPC-A defect is an instance of a class, so the class was checked. 23 variants carry an ISBN: 15 hold BOTH forms and match either way; 4 hold ONLY ISBN-10; 4 hold ONLY ISBN-13. So 8 of 23 book variants miss depending on which form the collector presents, and the two presentations differ systematically: a scanned book barcode encodes the ISBN-13, while the number printed on a copyright page is usually the ISBN-10. Lower impact than UPC-A (30 of 51 scannable barcodes) but the same shape: an identifier equivalence the lookup does not know about. WHY THE DATA FIX IS WRONG HERE, and this is the part worth recording: the obvious repair is to compute the missing form, since ISBN-13 is '978' plus the first 9 digits of the ISBN-10 plus a recomputed check digit. That would violate R11, which re-opens 20 sampled variants against their cited source and requires the IDENTIFIER to match. A computed ISBN-13 appears in no source; batch 02 recorded exactly what each Open Library record carried and several carry only one form, noted at the time. So the equivalence belongs at lookup alongside the GTIN rule, not in the catalogue. NOT added to the in-flight UPC-A dispatch, which is already scoped and holds the same files; over-scoped briefs have caused 5 turn-ceiling stops in this run. It follows that fix, in the same place, with the same end-to-end proof requirement. Note the conversion is a real algorithm rather than a prefix trick: ISBN-10's check digit is mod 11 and can be X.
2026-08-18T11:15:47Z | Identifier equivalence class CLOSED at two members (a useful negative result) | Having found the UPC-A gap and then the ISBN gap, the remaining identifier kinds were checked rather than assumed clean. Normalisation already absorbs every realistic variation in them: SLUS-00152, 'slus 00152', 'slus_00152' and 'SLUS00152' all resolve to slus00152; SLES-00024 and MOVATM205 behave the same. That covers how a person actually types a catalogue number off a spine. Those kinds have NO standard alternate encoding: a catalogue number, SKU, part number or matrix runout is the string the publisher printed and nothing else represents the same object. ONLY THE TWO INTERNATIONAL STANDARDS HAVE A SECOND LEGITIMATE FORM, which is not a coincidence: GTIN and ISBN are the two identifier systems in this catalogue that were redesigned mid-life, leaving two valid representations of one object in circulation. GTIN (UPC-A 12 against EAN-13 13) affects 30 of 51 scannable barcodes and is in flight; ISBN (10 against 13) affects 8 of 23 book variants and is queued behind it in the same files. THE BOUNDARY IS THE POINT: this is not 'identifier matching is unreliable', it is two specific standards with a documented dual encoding, both now known. Recorded so the verifier knows what NOT to re-derive as much as what to check.
2026-08-18T11:17:52Z | REAL DUPLICATE in the catalogue, and the UPC-A fix will expose it | Checking whether any cross-form barcode collision already exists found exactly one, and it is a genuine duplicate rather than a coincidence. IT ESCAPED DEDUPE THREE WAYS AT ONCE, which is why merge-on-dedupe did not catch it: the item titles differ only by a capital T in 'The' and identity comparison is case-sensitive; the item years differ because one recorded the REISSUE year 2021 and the other the compilation's original year 2001, and the settled rule is that two present-and-different years DO distinguish; and the barcodes differ only by the encoding, so the identifier could not reconcile them either. Any one of the three alone would have merged them. CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FIX IN FLIGHT: today these do not collide so a scan resolves to one variant; once the alternate-form lookup lands they WILL collide and render UNCERTAIN, showing a collector two candidates that are one record. Fixed by marking the slab data-stale before scanning and waiting for [data-testid=verdict]:not([data-stale]). GENERALISES TO ANY HARNESS THAT RE-CHECKS ON ONE PAGE, which includes the verdict-loop spec's 10 timed runs for F1 and its 10 offline checks for F2: if a selector is already satisfied when you start waiting, you are not waiting, you are reading history. Belongs with the other three capture and settling traps this run has paid for: fullPage hiding a real defect, a mid-wipe capture inventing one, and a stale server on port 3100 answering 200 with production headers while nothing hydrates.

WHY THE RULE MATTERS MORE THAN THE FIX: the catalogue will be rebuilt repeatedly as it grows toward R8's 220 items, and any spec pinning a minted id breaks on each rebuild while PRESENTING AS A PRODUCT FAILURE rather than test staleness. It was there the whole time, item RQKYNDEJG4 with ISBN 0761511067 on variant W9NFP6B0JM. A test that fails for a reason unrelated to the thing it tests is worse than no test, because it spends someone's attention on a false alarm and trains the next reader to discount it.

2026-08-18T12:21:05Z | C23's 'live text and never an image' has 3 failure modes, and each got a control | The C23 spec built a negative control for every distinct way the clause can fail, rather than one. (1) wordAsImage: an actual IMG element, rejected as a replaced element with no direct text node. (2) wordAsImageReplacement: a P containing the literal text 'OWNED' with text-indent -9999px and a background-image data URI, rejected on the indent and the background. THAT IS THE DANGEROUS ONE: the text is present in the DOM, textContent reads 'OWNED', and a naive assertion that the node has text PASSES while the user is looking at a picture. (3) wordAsSvgOutlines: text converted to vector paths. The distinction being drawn is between 'the node has text' and 'the text the user sees is live text', which is what the line actually asks. A verdict word rendered as an image would break at any font size, in any locale, under the pseudo-locale sweep and for a screen reader, and case 2 would sail past almost any check written without thinking about it first. Measurements at 390 and 1440 were still being written when this was recorded.

2026-08-18T12:23:04Z | F15 PROVEN: offline write, reconnect, replay, count query returns 1 | The mechanism has been described as idempotent since it was built, in the server route's own header, and had never been demonstrated. Now it has, against localhost:3200 with dev markers 0 and buildIdFoundInServedHtml true. OFFLINE WRITE: op 6463ebba-15c5-4155-b1af-8b6de75fdbf8, kind add_copy, outbox status 'pending', count query for that operation id 0 while offline, client_op rows unchanged at 76. So nothing reached the server and the write genuinely waited. REPLAY: 1 online event seen in the page, replay latency 263 ms, outbox status 'applied', COUNT QUERY FOR THAT OPERATION ID = 1, client_op rows 76 to 77. Exactly one row from exactly one write. BEYOND THE LINE'S LETTER, AND THE PART THAT MATTERS MOST: deliberateReplaysOfTheSameId = 2. The same operation id was sent TWICE and the count stayed at 1. That is what makes the outbox safe when a reconnect fires twice, which is what actually happens on a flaky connection in a shop, and it is the difference between an idempotency claim in a comment and an idempotency property that has been observed. THE ONE THAT LOOKED LIKE A DEFECT, and is not: c29-control-injected-flash-frame-1-WRONG-THEME.png is byte-identical to c29-control-injected-flash-frame-1-first-document.png and to the dark first-document frames of two OTHER runs. Frame 1 is simultaneously the first document frame AND the wrong-theme frame, so savedFrames writes the SAME capture under two keys. It matches the other runs' frame 1 because a full-viewport #0b1020 at 390x844 is the same bytes wherever it comes from. The control is sound: it produced a dark frame where light was required and the instrument caught exactly that one. NO DEFECT FOUND. Recorded because the next reader who hashes these files will land on the same alarming group, and the reasoning that clears it is not visible from the names. The 4 identical-by-design groups are also the answer to a fair question about C27 and C28: those lines are proven by images being the same, which is the one case where a duplicate is the evidence.

The cost is that an entry titled '(open)' stays titled '(open)' forever, and a reader working down the file reports closed defects as live ones. TWO DID EXACTLY THAT TODAY, and I only found them by re-measuring rather than re-reading. Both entries stay in the file unedited. DEFECTS.md is the index that stops them being misread: 9 closed and re-verified against the build today, 4 open and needing nobody but us, 4 open and waiting on the operator, and a section for the three lines that read like defects and are only early. Every row points at the entry or the file that carries the actual evidence; the index carries none of its own. IT ALSO STATES ITS OWN LIMIT: status in it is a claim by the lead, not a verdict, and a defect being closed is not the same as its rubric line passing. The verifier decides the second thing and this file must not be read as deciding it. D3 WAS RE-VERIFIED WHILE WRITING IT, and the subtle half holds: error.tsx, global-error.tsx and not-found.tsx all exist, and global-error.tsx sets className from a lazy useState reading the stored choice, so a light collector no longer gets a dark error page when React renders fresh into the existing document and wipes the class the head script had applied.
2026-08-18T12:54:08Z | F5 analysed: its zero is structural, not vacuous, and its first clause is closer than 'slice 5' suggests | F5 has two clauses and they are in very different states. CLAUSE 2, 'count of community photographs rendered in the OWNED frame is 0', currently measures 0 for two stacked reasons and only one of them survives slice 5. The vacuous reason is that no photograph exists anywhere yet. The REAL reason is structural: src/components/check/PhotoFrame.tsx reads only getPhoto(photoId) from the on-device store and renders a blob URL through next/image with unoptimized set, so there is no code path from that frame to a remote asset at all. Its own header comment states the rule (DESIGN.md note 1, F5) and the implementation matches it. Recording this NOW matters because when slice 5 lands the vacuous reason disappears and the structural guarantee is the only thing holding the clause up; a zero measured today would otherwise be re-read later as evidence about a world that no longer exists. CLAUSE 1, 'renders OWNED over the user's own cached photograph', is unreachable through the UI and NOT because the plumbing is missing. The data path is complete end to end: addCopy in src/lib/device/collection.ts lines 38 to 43 accepts input.photoBlob, mints an id, and writes a StoredPhoto to the photos store; getPhoto at line 213 reads it back; PhotoFrame renders it. What is absent is only the means to SUPPLY a blob: there is no file input anywhere in src/ and no capture-to-blob path, and every addCopy call site passes nothing, so collection.ts line 94 writes photoId null and PhotoFrame always renders the 45 degree no-photo hatch. CONSEQUENCE WORTH ACTING ON: F5's first clause does not have to wait for slice 5's upload UI. A spec can call addCopy with a blob directly, exactly as it would arrive from a picker, and prove the OWNED frame renders the user's own cached photograph. That is a real test of the rendering path rather than a stub, because the only thing it substitutes for is the picker. Whether that satisfies the line as written is the verifier's call, not mine, and I am not claiming it does.

It works from an EXPLICIT FILENAME LIST, so every new document is invisible to it until someone remembers to add it, and forgetting is silent. That already happened once in this run with VERIFIER-BRIEF.md. DEFECTS.md was about to be the second. Added it, then VERIFIED BY RUNNING THE SCRIPT rather than by re-reading the edit, which is the only reason the first omission was caught: a fresh snapshot at 20260818T125434Z now holds 14 files and DEFECTS.md is among them. THE DESIGN FLAW IS THE LIST ITSELF and I am not fixing it today. Globbing .forge/*.md would be self-maintaining, and it would also sweep in whatever a future agent leaves lying in that directory, which is a different failure with the same root: a snapshot that silently includes junk is no better than one that silently omits evidence. Either way the next new document is one forgotten line from being lost, and this entry is the record of the second time.

C16 requires every user-visible string to render transformed under the pseudo-locale, and a literal in JSX bypasses the catalogue entirely, so it passes every catalogue-based sweep and fails the line. TWO SCANS, BOTH CONTROLLED, both over src/components and src/app. Scan 1 looks for a text node on one line, >Text< , and returns 0. Scan 2 looks for a bare text line carrying no tag, no brace and no code punctuation, and returns 0 after excluding comments, imports, exports, const, return, type and interface. THE FIRST SCAN ALONE WOULD HAVE BEEN A FALSE CLEAN, and I only know that because I controlled it. grep is line-based, so a multi-line node written as <p> newline Hello world newline </p> never matches a >...< pattern; fed exactly that, scan 1 returns 0. Its control passed only because the control was single-line, which is the shape of mistake that makes a green meaningless. Scan 2 exists precisely to cover that case and its own control matches the multi-line form at line 2. So the result is 0 hardcoded user-visible strings, and it is a real 0 rather than the artifact of one pattern's blind spot. Also checked ScanControl.tsx specifically for user-facing attributes carrying literals (aria-label, title, placeholder, alt): none. Every string on that component resolves through t(), and 7 of the 9 scan keys are referenced there. WHAT THIS DOES NOT PROVE: that the pseudo-locale actually transforms them at runtime, which is C16's real subject and already carries 5 evidence entries. This is the static half, and its value is that it covers the code written since those entries were recorded.

2026-08-18T13:00:44Z | check:ui audits content it never asserts, and for two routes the content is a RACE | Following the dynamic-route finding I checked what content each audited route actually holds, and nearly published something false. WHAT I ALMOST CLAIMED: that every screen check:ui audits is empty, because each (theme x stored-choice x width) combination opens a FRESH browser context with no IndexedDB, and both list screens read the device store on mount rather than fetching. WHY THAT IS WRONG: CheckScreen fires a best-effort background sync on mount (CheckScreen.tsx lines 61 to 67, void syncCatalogueIndex().then(refreshDeviceState)), and routes are visited in sorted order on ONE page in ONE context, so / is visited first and can populate IndexedDB before /catalogue is ever loaded. One more file read is the only thing between the false claim and the real one. WHAT IS ACTUALLY TRUE, three different states and only one of them is stable. /collection is DEFINITIVELY EMPTY: CollectionListScreen reads listCopies() and nothing in the run calls addCopy, so no user copy exists in any combination. /collection/[publicId] is DEFINITIVELY NOT-FOUND: the segment is replaced with the literal check-ui-sample. / and /catalogue are RACE-DEPENDENT: whether the catalogue rows have landed depends on whether the background sync completed before the audit ran, and settle() waits for main, for header.topnav, for document.fonts.ready and then a FLAT 150 ms, with no assertion that the sync landed and no row count checked. THE SHARP POINT: this is not a scoping limitation, it is an instrument whose SUBJECT VARIES BETWEEN RUNS AND SAYS NOTHING ABOUT WHICH IT MEASURED. A long title that overflows at 390 px is caught only on runs where the sync happened to win the race, and a clean report from a run that lost it is indistinguishable from a clean report from a run that won. Every count taken from that sweep inherits the ambiguity, including C7's 0 violations across 96 combinations. CHEAPLY FIXABLE and I am not fixing it while the verifier holds the build: assert a row count before auditing, or seed the device store deterministically, so the audit states what it measured instead of whatever happened to be there. NOT MEASURED EMPIRICALLY YET: settling this needs a check:ui run, which performs a production build, and the verifier currently holds the tree.

2026-08-18T13:07:34Z | C26's 2 literal colours: the hand-written copies are gone, and the interpretation question is the verifier's | The verifier refused C26 on its second clause, 'count of literal colour values outside the token definitions is 0', and it was right: src/lib/theme/theme.ts held THEME_COLOR_DARK '#0b1020' and THEME_COLOR_LIGHT '#edf0f7' by hand, used in layout.tsx lines 29 to 30 and global-error.tsx lines 99 and 104. Count 2, not 0. The comment above them argued they were the one legitimate place for a colour in TypeScript. As the verifier put it, a comment calling them legitimate is not an exemption the rubric grants. THE REAL CONSTRAINT IS REAL: a meta theme-color with a media attribute is static document metadata that Next renders on the server, before any stylesheet is parsed and with no element to compute a style against, so it cannot read a custom property. Reading --g in the head script would work and would drop the no-JS path the media-query form gives for free. WHAT I DID: the value still reaches TypeScript, but by machine. scripts/gen/theme-colors.mjs parses --g out of :root and .lt in globals.css and writes src/lib/theme/theme-colors.generated.ts; theme.ts imports and re-exports it. globals.css is now the single AUTHORED source. NEGATIVE CONTROL, because a drift check that has never rejected anything is not a check: nudged --g from #0b1020 to #0b1021, ran check:theme-colors, got exit 1 and 'The ground colour moved in one place and not the other'; restored and got exit 0, with globals.css byte-identical to before, confirmed by an empty git diff. The old comment PROMISED these move when --g moves, which is a promise a person keeps; the check enforces it. Wired into check:all. tsc clean, eslint clean, npm test 111 pass 0 fail. THE PARSER IS DELIBERATELY NARROW, anchored to the selector at line start and taking only its block, because a looser regex over the whole file would happily return some other block's --g and produce a plausible WRONG colour, which is the exact failure this script exists to prevent. A BUG TSC CAUGHT AND I WOULD NOT HAVE: my first version used a bare re-export, export { X } from './y', which forwards the names without binding them locally, and theme.ts uses both internally at lines 47 and 94. Four TS2304 errors. A re-export is not an import. THE QUESTION I CANNOT DECIDE, and am not deciding: 2 literals still exist in src/, now in a file whose first line reads GENERATED FILE, DO NOT EDIT. A literal-counting sweep still counts 2. Whether a machine-derived copy sits inside 'the token definitions' is a reading of the rubric, and reading it in my own favour is the softening the harness forbids. C26 STAYS UNCHECKED. If the verifier says a generated copy still counts, the remaining option is moving the meta write into the head script and losing the no-JS media-query path, which is a trade the operator should make, not me.

FAULT 1, INCOMPLETE TEARDOWN. tests/sync-convergence.test.ts creates a franchise, a category and items, and its tearDown deletes variantIdentifier, verification, variant, catalogueItem and franchise. It never deleted the category, so sync-convergence-test-category was left behind by every run the test has ever made. FAULT 2, THE GUARD IS BLIND TO IT. check:no-fixture identifies test rows two ways, per its own header: franchise.slug or category.slug matching 'fixture-%', or catalogue_item.created_by matching 'fixture:%'. This category is named sync-convergence-test-category and the test's items carry created_by 'test:sync-convergence'. NEITHER PATTERN MATCHES. The one guard whose entire purpose is stopping test rows reaching production would pass a production database carrying this row. Measured, not inferred: over the 2 franchise rows and 15 category rows, 3 look like test artifacts by name and only 2 match the guard's prefix. FIXED, THE PART THAT IS MINE TO FIX: tearDown now deletes the category, and the slug is hoisted to a CATEGORY_SLUG constant beside FRANCHISE_SLUG so the creation site and the deletion site cannot drift. Verified by RUNNING the test, not by reading the edit: categories went 15 to 14 and sync-convergence categories 1 to 0, exit 0. The remaining 14 are 13 with items plus fixture-category, which the guard does see and correctly reports on a non-local target. NOT FIXED, BECAUSE THE RIGHT ANSWER IS A DESIGN DECISION: the guard's reach. Widening the pattern to 'test', 'sample' or 'dummy' would have caught this and would also flag a legitimate future category, since a real catalogue could hold something like screen-tests. The durable fix is marking test rows in the data rather than inferring them from a name, which is a schema change and not mine to make unilaterally. SAME SHAPE AS THE PATH CHECK LACKING A HISTORY TWIN: a guard whose reach is narrower than its purpose, passing green the whole time, correct about everything it looked at.

Settled is a STATE, not a synonym for raised or panelled. I read the geometry sentence, 'every settled surface carries it', as though settled meant any surface with a background, which is the reading that produces my finding. THE BUILD FITS THE STATE READING ALMOST EXACTLY, which is the evidence I should have weighed first. THE TELL IS .btn.sec. It is a bordered box on a background with no cut, specified that way. That is structurally the same thing as .card, so an uncut bordered container is not an omission in this system, it is a documented category. Under the state reading, .card is a neutral container and correctly uncut, and clause 2 may well be 0. WHAT I AM NOT DOING: flipping my claim to the opposite and calling that settled. The state reading is well supported and it is still a reading. THE STYLESHEET STATES ITS PRECEDENCE AND SCOPES IT NARROWLY: 'N is 8, 12 or 18 per DESIGN.md, EXCEPT THE VERDICT SLAB, whose cut sizes were measured directly off the rendered design in handoff-screen-01.md and take priority.' The exception names the verdict slab and nothing else. So .btn.pri at 8 is consistent with the rule as written, and my earlier reading, that handoff measurements take priority generally, applied the exception wider than its own sentence scopes it. THAT IS THE SECOND TIME IN AN HOUR I HAVE READ A RULE WIDER THAN IT IS WRITTEN, after reading DESIGN.md's 'every settled surface' as every raised panel. Both times the wider reading produced a finding and the written scope dissolved it. WHAT REMAINS TRUE AND UNRESOLVED: nothing in the codebase records a decision about the button. The comment explains the slab's 20px and is silent on whether 8 was chosen over the handoff's 10 deliberately or by following DESIGN.md without noticing the conflict. That is worth one sentence from whoever owns the design, and it is not worth me editing 8 call sites on my own reading. NOT A RUBRIC MATTER EITHER WAY: no line names cut sizes, and C8's scale governs margin and padding, not clip-path geometry.

2026-08-18T13:30:59Z | PRODUCT DEFECT: offline, an unknown address renders the check screen, and a comment in sw.js says otherwise | Found by the F16 re-measurement. WITH THE NETWORK DISABLED, an address the service worker holds no document for does not reach not-found.tsx. It renders the CHECK SCREEN at the requested URL: /no-such-route-f16 and a never-visited /collection/{id} both come back with heading 'Aisle check' and title 'Vitrine - The aisle check'. Online, the same two shapes correctly render 'That page is not here.' and 'This copy is not on this device.' THE COMMENT IN public/sw.js IS WRONG about its own behaviour. It says the shell fallback lets React hydrate 'this pathname's own client route'. It does not: the shell is the check screen's document, and React hydrates what the document contains rather than what the address implies. A comment describing intent was read as describing behaviour, by me among others, for the whole run. F16'S COUNT IS UNAFFECTED AND STAYS AT 0. The line counts routes rendering a BROWSER DEFAULT, and the check screen is a designed product screen. So this does not fail F16, which is exactly why it survived: every check that asked 'is this a browser error page' answered correctly. WHY IT MATTERS ANYWAY, under rule 2, market ready rather than merely working: a collector who mistypes an address or opens a stale link offline gets a working aisle check with no indication that the address they asked for does not exist. Nothing errors. Nothing is wrong on screen. They simply never learn the link was dead, and the URL bar still shows the address they typed. NOT FIXED. The fix is a decision about the offline shell: either the worker serves a not-found document for addresses it cannot match, or the shell reads its own location and routes client-side to the not-found state. Both are real changes to the offline boundary, which PLAN.md treats as load bearing, and neither should be made at the end of a long run without a measurement of what it costs the 161 ms cold-open figure F10 rests on.

FAULT 3, MINE, AND IT WOULD HAVE REPORTED A CLEAN ZERO. My first wiring read result.inheritColour and result.literalHex. A gate that cannot fire is worse than no gate, because it looks like coverage. Caught by grepping the audit's return statement instead of trusting the field names I had just typed. TWO THINGS FIXED WHILE THERE. The returned arrays were .slice(0, 10), so any count taken from them capped at 10 and 15 violations would have read as 10; counts are now returned whole beside the truncated samples, the way overflowCount already sat beside overflow. And backgroundWithoutColour is renamed surfaceWithoutColour, because the condition now includes font and a field named for half of what it measures is the next person's wrong assumption. Nothing else in the repository read either name, checked before renaming. All 3 eslint warnings in ui-audit.js are cleared, including the unused catch binding. EXPECT check:ui TO GO RED ON C26 AT THE NEXT RUN. That is the correct state: the line is unchecked and failing, and a red gate on a failing line is the point. It must not be softened to keep the suite green.

2026-08-18T13:50:34Z | A false negative that would have AGREED with the prior, and that is the dangerous kind | The capture agent's first visible-text counter scored the 318 px no-photo frame at 0 VISIBLE TEXT NODES. C20 was unchecked this afternoon on exactly that measurement: 318 x 238.5 px, 0 visible text nodes. So the reading would have been read as confirming a known defect, by me and by anyone else, and the fix would have been recorded as not working. IT WAS THE INSTRUMENT. The counter rejected any text with a CLIPPED ANCESTOR. The OWNED slab carries a 20 px corner cut and a fully open wipe inset, and neither covers a single glyph, but both are clip paths on an ancestor. The blanket rejection was replaced with sampled hit testing against elementsFromPoint, and the two paragraphs then scored 21 of 21 sample points each. THIS IS THE MOST DANGEROUS SHAPE THIS RUN HAS PRODUCED. Every other instrument error today announced itself by disagreeing with something: a count that did not match a total, a green from an empty set, a figure that contradicted a document. This one AGREED WITH THE EXPECTED ANSWER. A wrong measurement that confirms what you already believe has nothing to catch it, because the reflex on a surprising number is to re-check and the reflex on a confirming number is to move on. It was caught only because the agent was measuring a state it had just been told was fixed, so the confirmation was the surprise. THE CONTROLS NOW ENCODE IT, and this is why they are worth the tokens: an .srOnly-only box returns 0 visible with the reasons '1.0x1.0 box with overflow:hidden' and 'painted at 0 of 21 sample points'; a plain paragraph returns 1; a genuinely empty box returns 0; text clipped by a 20 px overflow-hidden box returns 0 with shown fraction 0.1429. Four controls spanning both answers, run through the same function in the same page as the real measurement. A counter that can only be shown to return 0 is not a counter. SECOND INSTRUMENT FAULT IN THE SAME PASS, found separately: both subjects sat below the 844 px fold, so the first captures did not contain what they claimed. That one I caught by opening the PNG.
2026-08-18T13:59:01Z | The verifier's report has now been lost twice, and the state on disk is the only reliable channel | Both verifier dispatches ended with the agent's OPENING LINE returned as its result and no report. First time: 'I'll start by reading the verifier brief completely.' Second time: 'Let me look at the approved comp and the new captures side by side.' Both runs did real work, 44 and 48 tool calls, 138k and 110k tokens. The first one's report arrived only after I asked for it explicitly, and it was excellent. WHAT I CHECKED INSTEAD OF ASSUMING, and it is the part worth keeping: .forge/DOD.md mtime is 15:12:46, which is the FIRST run's write. So the second run flipped nothing, and the 22 checks are the same 22. That distinguishes 'the verifier concluded nothing new is proven' from 'the verifier's write was lost', which are very different facts and look identical from a missing report. The file system settled it in one stat call. THE RULE THIS LEAVES: an agent's returned text is a convenience, and the state it wrote is the evidence. DOD.md's mtime and its checkbox list are the verifier's real output; the prose is commentary that happens to be useful. When the two disagree, or when one is missing, the disk wins. I have asked for the report a second time and told the agent the channel is unreliable, so it should send substance in the reply rather than expecting a later one to carry it.

So its measurements ran TEN MINUTES AFTER the build on disk stopped being the one it names. WHAT HAPPENED: it started next start against .next, then ran check:all, which invokes check:ui, which performs a production build unless UI_CHECK_SKIP_BUILD is set. The rebuild landed underneath its already-running server, which kept serving the old build from its loaded state. Its assertion 'served build id matched disk' was TRUE WHEN MADE AND FALSE BY MEASUREMENT TIME. It also reported 'I rebuilt nothing', which is wrong; check:all rebuilt for it. THIS IS EXACTLY THE PORT 3100 FAILURE, the one that invalidated F12, F13 and F16 and sent them for a second and now a third measurement. It recurred, to the verifier, during the run in which it was checking the lines that failure had invalidated. A one-time assertion at startup cannot see a rebuild that happens afterwards. IT COST NOTHING THIS TIME, and I checked before saying so rather than after: no file under src or public was modified between 15:46 and 15:53, and none since. YzDsr6hHAGTXS-1fwnrSm is a rebuild of BYTE-IDENTICAL SOURCE, since Next mints a fresh id on every build regardless. So the verifier measured the current product and recorded a stale name for it. Bookkeeping, not measurement. THE SAME EVENT WITH ONE SOURCE EDIT WOULD HAVE SILENTLY INVALIDATED SIX RE-MEASUREMENTS and produced six confident passes on a product that no longer existed, with nothing in any artifact to reveal it. THE FIX, now in the running dispatch: assert the build id at the START and again AFTER THE LAST MEASUREMENT, comparing served against disk both times, and record both readings. If they diverge the numbers are still good for the build served, and the artifact must name that build rather than whatever is on disk when it is written.

2026-08-18T14:42:15Z | Fixed the trap that silently destroys a good artifact, with a four-way control | The re-measurement agent found that test.afterAll writes an artifact whether the tests passed or failed, so a spec that dies early still writes one, built from whatever it managed to collect. Its own deliberate wrong-build-pin control OVERWROTE a complete f12-offline-routes.json with a two-key stub, and the run that destroyed it reported success, because writing the stub was the last thing it did. It caught this and re-ran clean. WHY IT IS WORSE THAN A LOST FILE: the stub is well-formed JSON with the right name in the right directory. Nothing downstream can tell it from a real record, the coverage map counts the line as evidenced, and a verifier reading it finds a short artifact rather than a missing one. The failure mode is a CONFIDENT SMALLER TRUTH replacing a complete one. THE FIX, in tests/e2e/harness.ts writeEvidence: an artifact with fewer top-level keys than the one already on disk is REFUSED with both counts named, and VITRINE_EVIDENCE_OVERWRITE=1 replaces one on purpose. Deliberately crude, and deliberately not a merge: merging would invent a record no single run produced, which is worse than either file. FOUR-WAY CONTROL, run against a scratch path so no real artifact was touched. A complete 4-key artifact writes. A 1-key stub is REFUSED, naming 4 to 1. A richer 5-key artifact is allowed, so the guard blocks shrinkage and not writing. The flag overwrites, leaving 1 key, so the escape hatch works. A guard proven only to refuse is not proven to permit. tsc clean. NOTE THE SHAPE, because it is the fourth today: the destructive act was performed BY A CONTROL. The wrong-build-pin control was correct, necessary, and the thing that broke the artifact. Instruments that prove a failure mode have to be as carefully sandboxed as the measurements they protect.
2026-08-18T14:43:56Z | The build-id gap is closed in the harness, and the agent built it better than I was about to | I had queued a re-assertion helper for tests/e2e/harness.ts after the verifier's server was measured against a build that had moved underneath it. The re-measurement agent had already built it, and its version separates a distinction mine would have collapsed. WHAT EXISTS NOW: assertServedBuildId(request, baseURL, expected, when) returns servedBuildId, buildIdOnDisk, expectedBuildId, and TWO SEPARATE BOOLEANS, servedMatchesExpected and servedMatchesDisk. My sketch had one comparison. Two is right, because those are different failures: served-against-expected catches a stale id in a dispatch, which is what I handed this agent; served-against-disk catches a rebuild landing under a running server, which is what happened to the verifier. Collapsing them into 'the target is correct' loses which one broke. The when parameter labels each call, and f12-offline-routes.spec.ts calls it three times: 'before any F12 clause', 'AFTER THE LAST F12 CLAUSE', and once more for its negative control. THE AFTER CALL IS THE WHOLE POINT. A one-time assertion at startup cannot see a rebuild that happens afterwards, and that is precisely how the verifier's 'served matches disk' was true when made and false by the time it measured. THE READER, TOO: servedBuildIdFrom reads the id out of the SERVED BYTES, from the flight payload, never from disk, so it reports what ANSWERED rather than what is currently sitting in .next. Its own comment records that the first version read for unescaped quotes, matched nothing, and returned null on a healthy document, and that it failed loudly rather than confirming, which is the only reason it was caught. It now tolerates both forms and its controls are recorded. Also present and used: measureSubjectInCapture and assertSubjectInCapture, which check that a capture's evidenced element is inside the frame. That closes the other failure from this afternoon, where an F6 capture stopped 27 px above the clauses it was evidence for. NOTHING FOR ME TO ADD. Recording it so the queued item is not done twice, and because the better design came from the agent that had just been bitten by the problem.

2026-08-18T14:56:17Z | F14 PROJECTED, not measured: 400 KB is not a constraint this catalogue can reach | F14 is refused because the line says 'at the shipped catalogue size' and the catalogue holds 145 variants against R8's 380. THE MEASURED POINT, from the verifier's own re-measurement: 145 variants, 56,843 B raw, 8,528 B gzip -9. That is 58.8 B per variant compressed. LINEAR PROJECTION TO 380 VARIANTS: 22,349 B, 21.8 KB, against a 400 KB cap. An 18.3x margin. The index would need roughly 6,964 VARIANTS to reach the cap, which is 18 times the full planned catalogue. WHY LINEAR IS THE CONSERVATIVE DIRECTION HERE: the payload is an array of similar rows, and gzip finds more repetition as such a corpus grows, so the real figure at 380 should come in UNDER the projection rather than over it. A projection that errs toward the cap is the safe kind. WHAT THIS IS FOR: slice 2 triples the catalogue and someone will reasonably ask whether the offline index budget survives it. It does, with an order of magnitude to spare, and that answer costs one division rather than a planning discussion. It also means F14 needs no design work, no shrinking and no format change: when the catalogue reaches 380 the line is measured once and closes. WHAT IT IS NOT: a pass. F14 requires the shipped size and the shipped size does not exist yet. If slice 2 changes what the index CARRIES per row rather than only how many rows it has, the 58.8 B figure moves and this projection is void.
2026-08-18T14:59:22Z | My evidence guard defended against the incident, not the class, and a verifier named the hole | I built writeEvidence's guard this afternoon after a control overwrote a complete F12 artifact with a two-key stub. It compared TOP-LEVEL KEY COUNTS. The verifier tested it rather than accepting it and found the hole: an artifact with the same keys and a hollowed body passes unchallenged. Cut a clauses object from 5 entries to 1, keep every key, and a key count says nothing. DEMONSTRATED, not argued: a record with identical top-level keys and clauses reduced 5 to 1 has the SAME key count, so the old guard permits it, and a recursive leaf count reads 3 against 7 and refuses it. SECOND FAULT, THE SAME SHAPE AS THE BUG THE GUARD EXISTS TO CATCH. VITRINE_EVIDENCE_OVERWRITE was a boolean, so setting it disarmed the guard for EVERY artifact the run wrote rather than the one the author meant. A blanket escape hatch on a guard against accidental destruction is an accident waiting on a different line. It now takes a FILENAME. Controlled four ways: the exact name is allowed, a DIFFERENT name is still guarded, * is allowed for the rare case of meaning all of them, and unset is guarded. BOTH FIXES ARE THE VERIFIER'S DESIGN. It named the recursive count and the named-file scoping, and it flagged that neither was blocking anything today, which is the difference between a critique and a demand. I would not have found either: I tested my guard against the failure I had just seen, which is exactly the corpus-narrower-than-the-question error this run keeps producing, applied to my own instrument. ALSO CORRECTED, ITS CATCH ON MY WRITING: I described F16 as '9 offline documents'. The artifact carries 6 route documents and 3 boundaries and keeps those counts apart deliberately; 9 appears nowhere in it. The substance was right and the number was a sum I made, which is the smaller cousin of the same habit.

2026-08-18T16:08:54Z | C12's missing transition register is a real decision, and the build already wrote down why it made the other choice | The verifier refused C12 clause 2 because the count of off-spec transitions is 0 only because the product has NO transitions, and DESIGN.md declares a register it does not have: line 72 'everything travels 140ms' and line 78 'Every other transition is a 140ms wipe'. THE BUILD DID NOT OVERLOOK THIS. globals.css lines 396 to 405 carry the decision and its reasoning verbatim: 'Deliberately no color/background-color transition utility here. C14 counts animated colour transitions during a theme swap at 0, and the theme swap is a synchronous class toggle with nothing else listening for it; a general-purpose transition on a token-driven property would also fire on that toggle. Nothing in this slice needs a colour transition badly enough to risk it.' THE TENSION IS REAL AND IT IS BETWEEN RUBRIC LINES. C12 wants a 140 ms register to exist. C14 wants 0 animated colour transitions during a theme swap. C29, WHICH IS CHECKED, wants 0 frames of the wrong theme on a cold load. A token-driven colour transition fires on the theme class toggle, so the naive way to satisfy C12 puts a checked line at risk. THEY ARE NOT STRICTLY CONTRADICTORY: C14 and C29 govern the theme swap and first paint, and a transition scoped to properties that are not token-driven, or disabled around the toggle, satisfies both. That is a real technique and it is more than a lint fix. WHAT I AM NOT DOING: adding transitions at the end of a long run to move one unchecked line, when the mechanism is exactly the one the build documented itself avoiding, and when getting it wrong costs C29, which is checked and was expensive. C12 stays refused and the gap is honest. THE DECISION, for a designer or the operator: either build the declared register with the theme swap explicitly excluded, and re-measure C14 and C29 afterwards rather than assuming, or amend DESIGN.md so it stops declaring a register the product does not want. The one thing that must not happen is the current state persisting silently, where the design says everything travels 140 ms and nothing does.

2026-08-18T16:13:55Z | Corrected a comment in sw.js that described behaviour the code does not have | public/sw.js line 96 claimed the shell fallback lets React hydrate 'this pathname's own client route from there, since none of the offline routes read server data in their read path'. Measured today and recorded twice, by two separate agents: IT DOES NOT. The shell IS the check screen's document, and React hydrates what the document CONTAINS rather than what the address IMPLIES, so /no-such-route and a never-visited /collection/{id} both come back reading 'Aisle check' with the requested address still in the URL bar. Online the same two addresses render 'That page is not here.' and 'This copy is not on this device.' WHY A COMMENT IS WORTH FIXING WHEN THE CODE IS NOT BEING CHANGED. This one asserted a behaviour, in the present tense, at the exact line that produces the opposite, and it is the first thing anyone reads when they arrive at this fallback. I believed it for most of today. A wrong comment about behaviour is worse than no comment, because it substitutes for the measurement that would have caught it: the reason nobody checked is that the file said what it did. The replacement states what was measured and when, states that F16 counts BROWSER DEFAULTS and a designed screen is not one, so the count stands at 0 and this is not a rubric failure, and states why it is still wrong for a collector who follows a stale link offline and gets a working screen with no sign the address was dead.
